A predictive ant colony optimization routing protocol for correlated link stability in wireless body area networks
Authors: Ben Othman Soufiane, Naima Saeed
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
BackgroundWireless Body Area Networks (WBANs) are essential for continuous health monitoring but face critical challenges due to dynamic link instability caused by body movements and strict bio-safety requirements. The fluctuating wireless channel leads to high packet loss, while radio frequency (RF) energy absorption, measured as Specific Absorption Rate (SAR), must remain below 1.6 W/kg to prevent tissue damage. Existing routing protocols often react to failures rather than predict them and rarely co-optimize reliability with thermal safety.MethodsThis paper proposes PACO-CLS (Predictive Ant Colony Optimization with Correlated Link Stability), a novel routing protocol that proactively addresses both network dynamics and bio-safety. It leverages tri-axial accelerometer data from sensor nodes to classify body posture using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM). This posture state conditions a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model, which predicts future link quality metrics (SNR, distance, SAR) over a 0.5-second horizon. These predictions drive an enhanced Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) algorithm, where path selection is guided by a multi-objective cost function. Crucially, the pheromone evaporation rate is dynamically modulated by the cumulative SAR, calculated via the Pennes bio-heat equation, to enforce thermal safety.ResultsExtensive simulations in NS-3.40, calibrated with empirical data from the MySignals HW V3 platform, demonstrate that PACO-CLS significantly outperforms QC-TriL, LEACH-WBAN, and Q-Learning-based routing. It achieves a network lifetime of 32.4 ± 1.2 hours (a 40.3% improvement), an energy efficiency of 17.8 ± 0.2 Mb/J, a packet delivery ratio of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$98.5\% \pm 0.3\%$$\end{document}, and end-to-end latency of 36.2 ± 2.1 ms. Critically, it maintains an average SAR of 0.82 ± 0.11 W/kg, well below the safety threshold across all tested scenarios, including high interference (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_d=0.3$$\end{document} sources/m2) and mobility (v=1.5 m/s).ConclusionsBy integrating predictive analytics with thermal-aware mechanisms, PACO-CLS establishes a new benchmark for intelligent WBAN routing. Its ability to proactively avoid unstable links based on correlated motion analytics and simultaneously enforce bio-safety constraints makes it a robust and clinically viable solution. PACO-CLS consistently meets stringent clinical requirements for real-time monitoring (PDR > 95%, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{e2e}$$\end{document} < 50 ms, SAR < 1 W/kg, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{life}} > 24$$\end{document} h), demonstrating its potential for next-generation, safe, and reliable healthcare applications.Supplementary InformationThe online version contains supplementary material available at https://doi.org/10.1186/s12911-026-03706-8.

Introduction

Wireless Body Area Networks (WBANs) represent a transformative paradigm in healthcare [1], enabling continuous, real-time monitoring of vital physiological parameters through wearable and implantable sensors [2]. These networks facilitate critical applications such as remote patient monitoring, early disease detection, and automated emergency response by reliably collecting and transmitting data, such as ECG, blood pressure, SpO, and glucose levels, to centralized hubs or cloud-based medical systems [3]. Operating under the IEEE 802.15.6 standard for on-body communication, WBANs utilize low-power, short-range wireless protocols to ensure compatibility with resource-constrained sensor nodes [4]. The stringent energy limitations of these devices necessitate highly efficient routing strategies to prolong network lifetime, which is crucial for long-term patient care [5]. Furthermore, WBANs must adhere to strict bio-safety regulations, as excessive radio frequency (RF) exposure can lead to tissue heating quantified by the Specific Absorption Rate (SAR), potentially causing harm if limits are exceeded [6]. The dynamic nature of the human body introduces significant challenges, including rapid link instability due to movement-induced fading, which compromises connectivity [7]. As healthcare increasingly depends on WBANs for personalized and proactive interventions, the demand for robust, energy-efficient, thermally-safe, and reliable communication protocols intensifies [8].

Routing in Wireless Body Area Networks presents significant challenges due to the dynamic and resource-constrained nature of on-body communication, where body movements cause severe link instability, leading to signal attenuation, fading, and topology changes that result in packet loss rates as high as 20–30% in active scenarios [9]. The limited battery capacity of sensor nodes necessitates highly energy-efficient routing to extend network lifetime, particularly for implantable devices where battery replacement is impractical [10], while strict bio-safety regulations require the Specific Absorption Rate (SAR) to remain below 1.6 W/kg to prevent RF-induced tissue heating [11]. High transmission latency, exceeding 200 ms, can compromise real-time health monitoring and delay critical medical interventions [12], and device heterogeneity further complicates routing decisions. Existing protocols such as LEACH-WBAN [13] improve energy efficiency but fail under dynamic conditions due to unreliable data delivery during motion, while tree-based approaches reduce latency but neglect energy balancing [14], leading to premature node failure; although bio-inspired algorithms like ACO adapt well to changing topologies through pheromone-based mechanisms [15], their WBAN-specific implementations (e.g., QC-TriL) often ignore thermal safety and posture-dependent link correlations [16]. Recent machine learning-enhanced methods offer improved link prediction but lack integration with SAR constraints [17], revealing a critical gap: the absence of a holistic framework that simultaneously optimizes energy, reliability, latency, thermal safety, and correlated link stability with low computational overhead suitable for constrained devices [18].

This paper proposes PACO-CLS (Predictive Ant Colony Optimization with Correlated Link Stability), a novel routing protocol designed to address the critical challenges of energy efficiency, link instability, and bio-safety in Wireless Body Area Networks. Building upon the bio-inspired ACO framework, where virtual ants explore paths and deposit pheromones to guide routing decisions, PACO-CLS introduces two key innovations: first, it leverages tri-axial accelerometer data from wearable sensors to classify body posture using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) [19], which conditions a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model to predict future link quality over a 0.5-second horizon, enabling proactive adaptation to motion-induced fading; second, it enforces thermal safety by dynamically modulating the pheromone evaporation rate based on cumulative Specific Absorption Rate (SAR), computed via the Pennes bio-heat equation, thereby diverting traffic from potential tissue heating hotspots. The protocol employs a multi-objective cost function that holistically optimizes energy efficiency for extended network lifetime, low end-to-end latency for real-time monitoring, high packet delivery ratio for reliability, and strict SAR compliance for user protection. By integrating predictive analytics with thermal-aware control, PACO-CLS achieves robust, scalable, and adaptive performance in dynamic environments, offering a transformative solution for safe and reliable next-generation healthcare applications.

The major contributions of PACO-CLS are:Predictive Link Stability Modeling with Correlated Motion Analytics: PACO-CLS introduces a novel predictive framework that forecasts link stability by analyzing correlated motion data from wearable sensor accelerometers. The protocol classifies body posture using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) based on features extracted from tri-axial accelerometer signals. This posture classification is then used to inform a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model that predicts the future quality of wireless links over a 0.5-second horizon, enabling proactive path selection before disruptions occur. By leveraging the spatial correlation of link quality across the body, where links in close anatomical proximity experience similar fading patterns due to shared motion, the protocol avoids unstable paths and reduces packet loss. This approach minimizes unnecessary retransmissions and energy waste, achieving up to a 97% packet delivery ratio in dynamic scenarios. It represents the first integration of such correlated motion analytics into ant colony optimization for WBANs, providing a scalable and adaptive solution for reliable real-time health monitoring.Thermal-Aware Routing for Bio-Safety Compliance: PACO-CLS pioneers the integration of thermal-aware routing within the ant colony optimization framework to ensure patient safety. The protocol computes the cumulative Specific Absorption Rate (SAR) for each node using a bio-heat transfer model that accounts for RF-induced tissue heating, metabolic heat generation, and blood perfusion cooling. To enforce compliance with the 1.6 W/kg SAR safety limit, the protocol dynamically modulates pheromone updates: paths passing through nodes with high cumulative SAR experience accelerated pheromone evaporation, effectively diverting traffic away from potential thermal hotspots. Simulations confirm that this mechanism successfully maintains tissue temperature increases below 1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{\circ}$$\end{document}C even under sustained transmission loads. This contribution directly addresses a critical gap in existing WBAN protocols, making PACO-CLS uniquely suitable for long-term operation of implantable and wearable medical sensors.Multi-Objective Optimization for Balanced Performance: PACO-CLS develops a sophisticated multi-objective heuristic that simultaneously optimizes energy efficiency, end-to-end latency, packet delivery reliability, and thermal safety. The routing decision process evaluates candidate paths based on multiple factors including residual node energy, transmission distance, current link reliability, predicted stability, and thermal impact. This holistic cost function ensures balanced network performance under the stringent constraints of WBANs. Furthermore, the protocol employs a fuzzy logic system to dynamically adjust the relative importance of each objective based on real-time conditions (e.g., prioritizing low latency during an emergency alert). This adaptability extends network lifetime by 28% and reduces end-to-end delay by 18% compared to state-of-the-art protocols, establishing a new benchmark for intelligent and versatile routing in heterogeneous sensor networks.Adaptive Pheromone Updates for Dynamic Topologies: PACO-CLS introduces an adaptive pheromone update mechanism that responds in real time to both posture-induced link variations and thermal constraints. Unlike traditional ACO protocols with static update rules, PACO-CLS uses real-time predictions from its VAR model and SAR feedback to accelerate pheromone evaporation on links that are predicted to become unstable or exceed thermal thresholds. This enables rapid convergence to new optimal paths as body movements change the network topology. The dynamic adaptation significantly improves resilience in high-mobility scenarios, reducing packet loss by 25%. This contribution establishes a new standard for adaptability in bio-inspired routing protocols, ensuring robust and reliable data delivery in the dynamic environment of a moving human body while maintaining low computational complexity suitable for resource-constrained nodes.

Predictive Link Stability Modeling with Correlated Motion Analytics: PACO-CLS introduces a novel predictive framework that forecasts link stability by analyzing correlated motion data from wearable sensor accelerometers. The protocol classifies body posture using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) based on features extracted from tri-axial accelerometer signals. This posture classification is then used to inform a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model that predicts the future quality of wireless links over a 0.5-second horizon, enabling proactive path selection before disruptions occur. By leveraging the spatial correlation of link quality across the body, where links in close anatomical proximity experience similar fading patterns due to shared motion, the protocol avoids unstable paths and reduces packet loss. This approach minimizes unnecessary retransmissions and energy waste, achieving up to a 97% packet delivery ratio in dynamic scenarios. It represents the first integration of such correlated motion analytics into ant colony optimization for WBANs, providing a scalable and adaptive solution for reliable real-time health monitoring.

Predictive Link Stability Modeling with Correlated Motion Analytics: PACO-CLS introduces a novel predictive framework that forecasts link stability by analyzing correlated motion data from wearable sensor accelerometers. The protocol classifies body posture using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM) based on features extracted from tri-axial accelerometer signals. This posture classification is then used to inform a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model that predicts the future quality of wireless links over a 0.5-second horizon, enabling proactive path selection before disruptions occur. By leveraging the spatial correlation of link quality across the body, where links in close anatomical proximity experience similar fading patterns due to shared motion, the protocol avoids unstable paths and reduces packet loss. This approach minimizes unnecessary retransmissions and energy waste, achieving up to a 97% packet delivery ratio in dynamic scenarios. It represents the first integration of such correlated motion analytics into ant colony optimization for WBANs, providing a scalable and adaptive solution for reliable real-time health monitoring.

Thermal-Aware Routing for Bio-Safety Compliance: PACO-CLS pioneers the integration of thermal-aware routing within the ant colony optimization framework to ensure patient safety. The protocol computes the cumulative Specific Absorption Rate (SAR) for each node using a bio-heat transfer model that accounts for RF-induced tissue heating, metabolic heat generation, and blood perfusion cooling. To enforce compliance with the 1.6 W/kg SAR safety limit, the protocol dynamically modulates pheromone updates: paths passing through nodes with high cumulative SAR experience accelerated pheromone evaporation, effectively diverting traffic away from potential thermal hotspots. Simulations confirm that this mechanism successfully maintains tissue temperature increases below 1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{\circ}$$\end{document}C even under sustained transmission loads. This contribution directly addresses a critical gap in existing WBAN protocols, making PACO-CLS uniquely suitable for long-term operation of implantable and wearable medical sensors.

Thermal-Aware Routing for Bio-Safety Compliance: PACO-CLS pioneers the integration of thermal-aware routing within the ant colony optimization framework to ensure patient safety. The protocol computes the cumulative Specific Absorption Rate (SAR) for each node using a bio-heat transfer model that accounts for RF-induced tissue heating, metabolic heat generation, and blood perfusion cooling. To enforce compliance with the 1.6 W/kg SAR safety limit, the protocol dynamically modulates pheromone updates: paths passing through nodes with high cumulative SAR experience accelerated pheromone evaporation, effectively diverting traffic away from potential thermal hotspots. Simulations confirm that this mechanism successfully maintains tissue temperature increases below 1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^{\circ}$$\end{document}C even under sustained transmission loads. This contribution directly addresses a critical gap in existing WBAN protocols, making PACO-CLS uniquely suitable for long-term operation of implantable and wearable medical sensors.

Multi-Objective Optimization for Balanced Performance: PACO-CLS develops a sophisticated multi-objective heuristic that simultaneously optimizes energy efficiency, end-to-end latency, packet delivery reliability, and thermal safety. The routing decision process evaluates candidate paths based on multiple factors including residual node energy, transmission distance, current link reliability, predicted stability, and thermal impact. This holistic cost function ensures balanced network performance under the stringent constraints of WBANs. Furthermore, the protocol employs a fuzzy logic system to dynamically adjust the relative importance of each objective based on real-time conditions (e.g., prioritizing low latency during an emergency alert). This adaptability extends network lifetime by 28% and reduces end-to-end delay by 18% compared to state-of-the-art protocols, establishing a new benchmark for intelligent and versatile routing in heterogeneous sensor networks.

Multi-Objective Optimization for Balanced Performance: PACO-CLS develops a sophisticated multi-objective heuristic that simultaneously optimizes energy efficiency, end-to-end latency, packet delivery reliability, and thermal safety. The routing decision process evaluates candidate paths based on multiple factors including residual node energy, transmission distance, current link reliability, predicted stability, and thermal impact. This holistic cost function ensures balanced network performance under the stringent constraints of WBANs. Furthermore, the protocol employs a fuzzy logic system to dynamically adjust the relative importance of each objective based on real-time conditions (e.g., prioritizing low latency during an emergency alert). This adaptability extends network lifetime by 28% and reduces end-to-end delay by 18% compared to state-of-the-art protocols, establishing a new benchmark for intelligent and versatile routing in heterogeneous sensor networks.

Adaptive Pheromone Updates for Dynamic Topologies: PACO-CLS introduces an adaptive pheromone update mechanism that responds in real time to both posture-induced link variations and thermal constraints. Unlike traditional ACO protocols with static update rules, PACO-CLS uses real-time predictions from its VAR model and SAR feedback to accelerate pheromone evaporation on links that are predicted to become unstable or exceed thermal thresholds. This enables rapid convergence to new optimal paths as body movements change the network topology. The dynamic adaptation significantly improves resilience in high-mobility scenarios, reducing packet loss by 25%. This contribution establishes a new standard for adaptability in bio-inspired routing protocols, ensuring robust and reliable data delivery in the dynamic environment of a moving human body while maintaining low computational complexity suitable for resource-constrained nodes.

Unlike traditional ACO protocols with static update rules, PACO-CLS uses real-time predictions from its VAR model and SAR feedback to accelerate pheromone evaporation on links that are predicted to become unstable or exceed thermal thresholds. This enables rapid convergence to new optimal paths as body movements change the network topology. The dynamic adaptation significantly improves resilience in high-mobility scenarios, reducing packet loss by 25%. This contribution establishes a new standard for adaptability in bio-inspired routing protocols, ensuring robust and reliable data delivery in the dynamic environment of a moving human body while maintaining low computational complexity suitable for resource-constrained nodes.

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows: Section “System Model and Design Objectives” introduces the system model and design objectives of PACO-CLS. Section “Proposed System” provides a comprehensive overview of the proposed system architecture and operational workflow. Section “Experimental Setup” details the experimental setups and performance metrics used for evaluation. Section “Performance Results and Discussion” presents and discusses the simulation performance results. Section “Real-Time Hardware Implementation” evaluates the real-time hardware implementation feasibility and statistical validation of the protocol. Finally, Section “Conclusion and Future Works” concludes the paper and outlines potential directions for future research.

System model and design objectives

This section presents a rigorous system model for the PACO-CLS protocol in a hierarchical Wireless Body Area Network framework, incorporating device, fog, and cloud layers. The model captures the stochastic nature of WBANs due to human mobility, employing probabilistic and bio-electromagnetic equations to ensure analytical depth. Formulations are grounded in IEEE 802.15.6 standards, with derivations for key metrics like correlated link stability and Specific Absorption Rate, enabling precise simulation and analysis. \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_0$$\end{document}Noise power spectral densitydBmΔ tRouting decision and prediction time intervals\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_k$$\end{document}Accumulated path cost for ant k–

Key symbols and parameters

Network architecture

To support reliable, low-latency, and bio-safe health monitoring, we propose a multi-tiered hierarchical architecture for Wireless Body Area Networks, integrating three distinct layers, Device, Fog, and Cloud, into a unified intelligent system. Figure 1 illustrates the end-to-end PACO-CLS system architecture, comprising (a) the Device Layer with MySignals HW V3 sensors and predictive routing at the Central Hub, (b) the Fog Layer for real-time filtering, compression, and low-latency queuing on Raspberry Pi 4, and (c) the Cloud Layer for secure storage, AI-driven analytics, and clinical alert generation. This architecture enables efficient data acquisition, context-aware routing, edge processing, and large-scale analytics while addressing critical WBAN-specific challenges: energy scarcity, dynamic link instability due to body motion, and thermal safety from radio frequency exposure.Fig. 1Overview of the PACO-CLS system architecture

Overview of the PACO-CLS system architecture

The overall network is modeled as a composite directed graph: 1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G = G_d(t) \cup G_f \cup G_c,$$\end{document}

where: - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_d(t) = (V_d(t), E_d(t)) $$\end{document}: time-varying graph of the device layer, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_f = (V_f, E_f) $$\end{document}: fog layer graph, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_c = (V_c, E_c) $$\end{document}: cloud layer graph.

Inter-layer communication is governed by inter-tier edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ E_{\mathrm{inter}} $$\end{document}, representing uplink transmissions, data aggregation, and control feedback. The end-to-end data propagation delay across the system is formulated as: 2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{\mathrm{total}} = D_d(t) + D_f + D_c + \sum_{e \in E_{\mathrm{inter}}} D_e,$$\end{document}

where: - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_d(t) $$\end{document}: intra-WBAN delay (highly dynamic due to body movements), - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_f $$\end{document}: fog processing and queuing delay, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_c $$\end{document}: cloud computation and response delay, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_e $$\end{document}: transmission, propagation, and contention delay over inter-layer links.

This section presents a comprehensive description of each architectural tier, including node models, communication protocols, energy consumption, thermal constraints, and computational workflows, laying the foundation for the operation of the proposed PACO-CLS routing protocol across the entire stack.

Device layer

The device layer comprises N sensor nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S = \{s_1, s_2, \dots, s_N\}$$\end{document} and a central hub h, forming a dynamic graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_d(t) = (V_d(t), E_d(t))$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_d(t) = S \cup \{h\}$$\end{document}. Edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_d(t)$$\end{document} exist if the received signal strength indicator (RSSI) exceeds a threshold γ, i.e., \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{RSSI}_{ij}(t) > \gamma$$\end{document}, influenced by path loss: 3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PL_{ij}(t) = PL_0 + 10n \log_{10}(d_{ij}/d_0) + X_\sigma(t),$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PL_0$$\end{document} is reference loss at distance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_0$$\end{document}, n is path loss exponent (body-specific, e.g., 3–5), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d_{ij}$$\end{document} is distance between nodes i and j, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_\sigma(t) \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$$\end{document} models shadowing from posture variations.

Energy consumption for node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_i$$\end{document} is detailed as: 4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_i(t) = E_{tx,i} + E_{rx,i} + E_{sens,i} + E_{proc,i},$$\end{document}

with transmission cost: 5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{tx,i} = (E_{elec} + E_{amp} \cdot d_{ij}^\alpha) \cdot b,$$\end{document}

where b is bit length, α = 2-4 (free-space or multi-path), reception: 6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{rx,i} = E_{elec} \cdot b,$$\end{document}

sensing: 7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{sens,i} = E_s \cdot r_s,$$\end{document} (rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_s$$\end{document}), and processing: 8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{proc,i} = E_p \cdot c,$$\end{document} (cycles c). Residual energy evolves as: 9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{res,i}(t+1) = E_{res,i}(t) - E_i(t) - E_{leak,i},$$\end{document}

incorporating leakage \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{leak,i}$$\end{document}.

Correlated link stability is predicted using a Markov chain model for posture states \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P = \{p_1, p_2, \dots, p_K\}$$\end{document} (e.g., standing, walking), with transition matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Pi = [\pi_{kl}]$$\end{document}, where: 10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi_{kl} = P(p(t+1)=p_l \mid p(t)=p_k).$$\end{document}

Link quality correlation is defined as: 11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_{ij} = \frac{\mathrm{Cov}(Q_i, Q_j)}{\sigma_{Q_i} \sigma_{Q_j}},$$\end{document}

with quality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_i(t) = f(p(t), d_i)$$\end{document}, yielding predictive stability: 12\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{corr,ij}(t+\Delta t) = \sum_{l=1}^{K} \pi_{kl} \cdot g(Q_{ij}|p_l),$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g(x) = (1 + e^{-\beta (x - \theta)})^{-1}$$\end{document} is a logistic function modeling reliability.

Thermal modeling employs the Pennes bio-heat equation: 13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_m c \frac{\partial T}{\partial t} = \nabla \cdot (k \nabla T) + \rho_b c_b \omega_b (T_b - T) + Q_m + Q_{SAR},$$\end{document}

where T is tissue temperature, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_m, c, k$$\end{document} are tissue density, specific heat, and conductivity, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_b, c_b, \omega_b, T_b$$\end{document} are blood perfusion parameters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_m$$\end{document} is metabolic heat, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{SAR} = \rho_m \cdot \mathrm{SAR}_{ij} = \sigma |\mathbf{E}_{ij}|^2$$\end{document}, with electric field magnitude: 14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|\mathbf{E}_{ij}| \propto \sqrt{\frac{P_{tx}}{d_{ij}^2}}.$$\end{document}

SAR thresholds ensure cumulative exposure remains safe: 15\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{\tau} \int_{t-\tau}^{t} \mathrm{SAR}(t\prime) dt\prime \leq \mathrm{SAR}_{\mathrm{max}}.$$\end{document}

PACO-CLS routes data multi-hop, integrating these models into ant heuristics.

Fog layer

The fog layer includes M edge nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F = \{f_1, f_2, \dots, f_M\}$$\end{document}, forming graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_f = (H \cup F, E_f)$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H = \{h_1, \dots, h_Q\}$$\end{document} are hubs from multiple WBANs. Interconnections use higher-power protocols, with link capacity: 16\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{fk} = B \log_2(1 + \mathrm{SNR}_{fk}),$$\end{document}

bandwidth B, SNR \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{SNR}_{fk} = P_{tx} / (N_0 + I)$$\end{document}, noise \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_0$$\end{document}, interference I.

Processing at fog node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_k$$\end{document} is modeled as an M/G/1 queueing system, with arrival rate: 17\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda_k = \sum_{h \in H_k} r_h,$$\end{document} (service rate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_h$$\end{document}), service time: 18\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_k = \frac{D_{\mathrm{size}}}{C_k} + T_{\mathrm{comp}},$$\end{document}

computational capacity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_k$$\end{document} (FLOPS), complexity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{comp}}$$\end{document}. Waiting time: 19\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_k = \frac{\lambda_k \mathbb{E}[S_k^2]}{2(1 - \rho_k)},$$\end{document}

utilization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_k = \lambda_k \mathbb{E}[S_k]$$\end{document}. Energy for fog is: 20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{f,k} = E_{comm,k} + E_{comp,k},$$\end{document}

with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{comp,k} = \kappa C_k^3 f_k^2$$\end{document}, dynamic voltage scaling factor κ, frequency \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_k$$\end{document}.

Thermal extension to fog considers heat dissipation: 21\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_k = \alpha E_{comp,k} + \beta P_{tx,k},$$\end{document}

with coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha, \beta$$\end{document}. PACO-CLS data aggregates here, with predictive models retrained using local datasets, e.g., updating Π via maximum likelihood estimation: 22\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\pi}_{kl} = \frac{n_{kl}}{\sum_{l\prime} n_{kl\prime}},$$\end{document}

transition counts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{kl}$$\end{document}. This layer enables low-latency feedback, such as rerouting commands back to device layer based on global posture patterns.

Cloud layer

The cloud layer encompasses P virtual machines \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C = \{c_1, c_2, \dots, c_P\}$$\end{document}, modeled as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G_c = (F \cup C, E_c)$$\end{document}, with high-bandwidth links: 23\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{c,p} = B_c \log_2(1 + \mathrm{SNR}_{c,p}),$$\end{document}

broader \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_c$$\end{document}. Resource allocation uses optimization: 24\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max_{\{r_p\}} \sum_p U_p, \quad \text{subject to } \sum_p r_p \leq R_{\mathrm{total}}, \quad U_p = \log(r_p),$$\end{document}

rates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r_p$$\end{document}.

Data processing employs machine learning models, e.g., LSTM for time-series prediction of health metrics from WBAN data, with loss: 25\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal{L} = \frac{1}{T} \sum_{t=1}^{T} (y_t - \hat{y}_t)^2,$$\end{document}

actual \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_t$$\end{document}, predicted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{y}_t$$\end{document}. Security is formalized via encryption overhead: 26\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$O_{enc} = \gamma_{enc} D_{\mathrm{size}},$$\end{document}

factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma_{enc}$$\end{document}.

Latency is decomposed as: 27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_c = L_{\mathrm{uplink}} + L_{\mathrm{proc}} + L_{\mathrm{downlink}},$$\end{document}

with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\mathrm{uplink}} = D_{\mathrm{size}} / C_{\mathrm{uplink}} + \tau_{\mathrm{prop}}$$\end{document}, propagation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau_{\mathrm{prop}}$$\end{document}. Cloud integrates PACO-CLS metrics for ensemble learning, e.g., federated updates to predictive models: 28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta_{\mathrm{global}} = \sum_k w_k \theta_k,$$\end{document}

weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_k$$\end{document} based on data volume. Thermal concerns are minimal but monitored via aggregate heat models. This layer enables scalable analytics, deriving long-term insights from correlated WBAN data across users.

Design objectives

The design of PACO-CLS is guided by a constrained multi-objective optimization framework that simultaneously maximizes network performance across four critical dimensions: energy efficiency, latency, reliability, and thermal safety. These objectives are formalized as a vector utility function: 29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbf{U} = \left[ U_1,\, U_2,\, U_3,\, U_4 \right]^T,$$\end{document}

where:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_1 = \min_i \left( E_{\mathrm{res},i}(T) / E_{\mathrm{init},i} \right) $$\end{document}: normalized minimum residual energy at time T , representing energy fairness and network lifetime,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_2 = 1 / \mathbb{E}[D_{\mathrm{e2e}}] $$\end{document}: inverse of average end-to-end delay, promoting timeliness for real-time health monitoring,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_3 = \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{PDR}] $$\end{document}: expected packet delivery ratio, capturing transmission reliability,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_4 = 1 / \max_{i,j} \left( \frac{1}{\tau} \int_{t-\tau}^{t} \mathrm{SAR}_{ij}(t\prime) dt\prime \right) $$\end{document}: inverse of peak averaged SAR, ensuring thermal safety compliance.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_1 = \min_i \left( E_{\mathrm{res},i}(T) / E_{\mathrm{init},i} \right) $$\end{document}: normalized minimum residual energy at time T , representing energy fairness and network lifetime,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_1 = \min_i \left( E_{\mathrm{res},i}(T) / E_{\mathrm{init},i} \right) $$\end{document}: normalized minimum residual energy at time T , representing energy fairness and network lifetime,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_2 = 1 / \mathbb{E}[D_{\mathrm{e2e}}] $$\end{document}: inverse of average end-to-end delay, promoting timeliness for real-time health monitoring,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_2 = 1 / \mathbb{E}[D_{\mathrm{e2e}}] $$\end{document}: inverse of average end-to-end delay, promoting timeliness for real-time health monitoring,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_3 = \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{PDR}] $$\end{document}: expected packet delivery ratio, capturing transmission reliability,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_3 = \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{PDR}] $$\end{document}: expected packet delivery ratio, capturing transmission reliability,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_4 = 1 / \max_{i,j} \left( \frac{1}{\tau} \int_{t-\tau}^{t} \mathrm{SAR}_{ij}(t\prime) dt\prime \right) $$\end{document}: inverse of peak averaged SAR, ensuring thermal safety compliance.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ U_4 = 1 / \max_{i,j} \left( \frac{1}{\tau} \int_{t-\tau}^{t} \mathrm{SAR}_{ij}(t\prime) dt\prime \right) $$\end{document}: inverse of peak averaged SAR, ensuring thermal safety compliance.

Due to the conflicting nature of these objectives (e.g., minimizing delay may increase energy consumption), we seek Pareto-optimal solutions via weighted scalarization: 30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U = \sum_{k=1}^{4} w_k U_k, \quad w_k > 0, \quad \sum_{k=1}^{4} w_k = 1,$$\end{document}

where weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ w_k $$\end{document} reflect application-specific priorities and are adaptively tuned using fuzzy logic in PACO-CLS.

The optimization is subject to the following hard constraints: 31\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ E_{\mathrm{res},i}(t) \geq E_{\mathrm{min}} \forall i, t \quad \text{(Minimum energy reserve)} $$\end{document}32\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \frac{1}{\tau} \int_{t-\tau}^{t} \mathrm{SAR}_{ij}(t\prime) dt\prime \leq \mathrm{SAR}_{\mathrm{max}} = 1.6~\mathrm{W/kg} \forall i,j,t \quad \text{(IEEE C95.1 compliance)} $$\end{document}33\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P_{\mathrm{corr},ij}(t) \geq P_{\mathrm{min}} \forall (i,j) \in E_d(t) \quad \text{(Link stability threshold)} $$\end{document}34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ D_{\mathrm{e2e}} \leq D_{\mathrm{max}} = 200~\mathrm{ms} \quad \text{(Real-time QoS requirement)}$$\end{document}

PACO-CLS solves this problem using an enhanced Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) framework. The heuristic desirability of a link (i,j) is defined as: 35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta_{ij} = \underbrace{\frac{E_{\mathrm{res},j}}{E_{\mathrm{init},j}}}_{\mathrm{Energy}} +\underbrace{\frac{1}{d_{ij}}}_{\mathrm{Distance}} +\underbrace{R_{ij}}_{\mathrm{Reliability}} +\underbrace{\frac{1}{\mathrm{SAR}_{ij}}}_{\text{Thermal safety}} +\underbrace{P_{\mathrm{corr},ij}}_{\mathrm{Stability}},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ R_{ij} = 1 - \mathrm{BER}_{ij} $$\end{document} is the link reliability, and bit error rate is modeled as: 36\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{BER}_{ij} = Q\left( \sqrt{2 \cdot \mathrm{SNR}_{ij}} \right), \quad Q(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}} \int_x^\infty e^{-u^2/2} du.$$\end{document}

Pheromone dynamics are governed by:

Initialization: 37\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau_{ij}(0) = \frac{1}{N \cdot L_{\mathrm{avg}}},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ L_{\mathrm{avg}} $$\end{document} is the average path length.

Update rule: 38\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau_{ij} \leftarrow (1 - \rho) \tau_{ij} + \sum_{k} \Delta \tau_{ij}^k,$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Delta \tau_{ij}^k = Q / C_k $$\end{document} if link (i,j) lies on ant k ’s path, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ C_k $$\end{document} is the path cost: 39\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_k = \alpha_1 D_k + \alpha_2 E_k + \alpha_3 (1 - \mathrm{PDR}_k) + \alpha_4 \mathrm{SAR}_k,$$\end{document}

with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \alpha_i > 0 $$\end{document} balancing delay, energy, reliability, and thermal impact.

To enforce thermal safety, a thermal-aware pheromone evaporation mechanism is introduced: 40\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho_{\mathrm{th},ij} = \rho \left( 1 + \kappa \cdot \frac{\mathrm{SAR}_{ij}}{\mathrm{SAR}_{\mathrm{max}}} \right),$$\end{document}

where ρ is the base evaporation rate and κ > 0 amplifies evaporation on thermally critical links, discouraging their use.

Convergence of PACO-CLS is analyzed using stochastic approximation theory, which guarantees that under mild ergodicity and persistence of excitation conditions: 41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lim_{t \to \infty} \mathbb{P}(\text{optimal path is selected}) = 1.$$\end{document}

This formal optimization framework ensures that PACO-CLS achieves a balanced, adaptive, and bio-safe routing solution tailored to the stringent and multi-faceted demands of Wireless Body Area Networks.

Proposed system

This section delineates the system in all steps.The steps span from initial configuration to cloud reception, ensuring comprehensive coverage of data flow, prediction, routing, and analytics. Formulations incorporate IEEE 802.15.6 constraints, with derivations for key metrics like correlated stability and SAR, enabling analytical tractability and simulation fidelity.

Model and system configuration

The initialization of the Wireless Body Area Network model begins with the formulation of a time-varying dynamic graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ G_d(t) = (V_d(t), E_d(t)) $$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ V_d(t) = \{s_1, s_2, \dots, s_N, h\} $$\end{document} denotes the set of N heterogeneous sensor nodes and a central hub h . This graph captures the dynamic network topology induced by human body movements, with nodes placed on or within the body to monitor vital signs. An edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ (i,j) \in E_d(t) $$\end{document} exists between nodes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ s_i $$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ s_j $$\end{document} if the link quality metric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_{ij}(t) $$\end{document} exceeds a predefined threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_{\min} $$\end{document}, i.e., 42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_{ij}(t) \geq Q_{\min},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q_{ij}(t) = \mathrm{SNR}_{ij}(t) - PL_{ij}(t) $$\end{document} combines signal-to-noise ratio and path loss to reflect effective communication capability.

The path loss \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ PL_{ij}(t) $$\end{document} is modeled with high fidelity to on-body propagation characteristics: 43\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$PL_{ij}(t) = PL_0 + 10n \log_{10}\left(\frac{d_{ij}(t)}{d_0}\right) + X_\sigma(t) + F(t),$$\end{document}

where: - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ PL_0 = 40 $$\end{document} dB is the reference path loss at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ d_0 = 1 $$\end{document} m, - n = 4.2 is the empirically derived path loss exponent for on-body channels in tissue environments, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ d_{ij}(t) $$\end{document} is the time-varying Euclidean distance between nodes, computed from 3D body coordinates \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf{r}_i(t) $$\end{document} updated via kinematic integration: 44\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf{r}_i(t) = \mathbf{r}_i(0) + \int_0^t \mathbf{v}_i(s) \, ds, $$\end{document}

with velocity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \mathbf{v}_i(s) $$\end{document} derived from tri-axial accelerometer data, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ X_\sigma(t) \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2) $$\end{document} models log-normal shadowing due to posture changes, with σ = 5 - 8 dB depending on activity, - F(t) represents fast fading, modeled using the Nakagami-m distribution with PDF [19]: 45\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ f_F(x) = \frac{2m^m x^{2m-1}}{\Gamma(m) \Omega^m} \exp\left(-\frac{m x^2}{\Omega}\right), $$\end{document}

where m ∈ [1,3] controls fading severity ( m=1 : Rayleigh, m=3 : near-Rayleigh), and Ω is the spread parameter.

Energy model

Energy consumption follows the first-order radio model. For transmitting b bits over distance d , the energy is: 46\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\mathrm{tx}}(b, d) = b \cdot E_{\mathrm{elec}} + b \cdot E_{\mathrm{amp}} \cdot d^\alpha,$$\end{document}

where: - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ E_{\mathrm{elec}} = 50 $$\end{document} nJ/bit: circuit energy for baseband processing, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ E_{\mathrm{amp}} = 100 $$\end{document} pJ/bit/m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^2$$\end{document}: power amplifier coefficient, - α = 2 for free-space (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ d^{-2} $$\end{document}) and α = 4 for multi-path (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ d^{-4} $$\end{document}) propagation.

Reception energy is: 47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\mathrm{rx}} = b \cdot E_{\mathrm{elec}}.$$\end{document}

Residual energy at node \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ s_i $$\end{document} evolves as: 48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\mathrm{res},i}(t+1) = E_{\mathrm{res},i}(t) - E_{\mathrm{tx},i}(t) - E_{\mathrm{rx},i}(t) - E_{\mathrm{sens},i}(t) - E_{\mathrm{proc},i}(t).$$\end{document}

Thermal safety and SAR constraints

To ensure bio-safety, the Specific Absorption Rate is constrained as: 49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\frac{1}{V} \int_V \frac{\sigma(\mathbf{r}) |\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{r})|^2}{\rho(\mathbf{r})} \, dV \leq \mathrm{SAR}_{\mathrm{max}} = 1.6~\mathrm{W/kg},$$\end{document}

averaged over V = 1 - 10 g of tissue. Here: - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma(\mathbf{r}) \in [0.1, 1] $$\end{document} S/m: electrical conductivity, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \rho(\mathbf{r}) \in [1000, 1100] $$\end{document} kg/m\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$^3$$\end{document}: tissue density, - \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{r})| \propto P_{\mathrm{tx}} / d_{ij}^2 $$\end{document}: electric field strength.

Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) configuration

PACO-CLS employs an enhanced ACO framework with the following hyperparameters:Pheromone influence: α = 1 ,Heuristic weight: β = 2 ,Evaporation rate: ρ = 0.1 ,Ant count: A = 10N , scaling with network size to ensure sufficient path sampling.

Pheromone influence: α = 1 ,

Pheromone influence: α = 1 ,

Heuristic weight: β = 2 ,

Heuristic weight: β = 2 ,

Evaporation rate: ρ = 0.1 ,

Evaporation rate: ρ = 0.1 ,

Ant count: A = 10N , scaling with network size to ensure sufficient path sampling.

Ant count: A = 10N , scaling with network size to ensure sufficient path sampling.

The convergence probability, derived from coupon collector theory, ensures: 50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\mathrm{conv}} \approx 1 - e^{-A / N} > 99\% \quad \text{for } N=10.$$\end{document}

The initial pheromone level is: 51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau_{ij}(0) = \frac{1}{N \cdot \bar{d}}, \quad \bar{d} = \frac{1}{|E_d(0)|} \sum_{(i,j) \in E_d(0)} d_{ij},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \bar{d} \approx 0.3 $$\end{document} m is the average inter-node distance. This ensures uniform initial path selection probabilities: 52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p_{ij}(0) = \frac{\tau_{ij}(0)^\alpha \eta_{ij}(0)^\beta}{\sum_{l \in N_i} \tau_{il}(0)^\alpha \eta_{il}(0)^\beta} \approx \frac{1}{|N_i|},$$\end{document}

with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ |N_i| $$\end{document} being the degree of node i .

Posture modeling via Markov Chain

Body posture is modeled as a discrete-time Markov chain [20] with state space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ P = \{p_k\}_{k=1}^K $$\end{document}, K=5 (e.g., standing, sitting, walking, running, lying). The transition matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Pi = [\pi_{kl}] $$\end{document} is estimated via maximum likelihood: 53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\pi}_{kl} = \frac{n_{kl}}{\sum_{l\prime=1}^K n_{kl\prime}},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ n_{kl} $$\end{document} counts transitions from posture \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_k $$\end{document} to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ p_l $$\end{document} in historical sequences of length L . Confidence intervals (95%) are: 54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\pi}_{kl} \pm 1.96 \sqrt{\frac{\hat{\pi}_{kl}(1 - \hat{\pi}_{kl})}{\sum_l n_{kl}}}.$$\end{document}

The stationary distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \boldsymbol{\pi}^\infty $$\end{document}, representing long-term posture occupancy, solves: 55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\boldsymbol{\pi}^\infty = \boldsymbol{\pi}^\infty \Pi,$$\end{document}
