Scalability performance of PACO-CLS under increasing network size

The proposed hierarchical clustering mechanism is strictly enforced for N > 30 and becomes essential for N = 100 . By partitioning the 100 nodes into C = 10 clusters and offloading global route computation to fog nodes, the protocol limits ant exploration to local clusters. This architectural shift maintains CPU utilization at 41.8% for N = 100 , ensuring stable operation on low-power microcontrollers without violating the 100 ms decision interval. Furthermore, cluster-based power control significantly reduces inter-node interference, improving the average PDR by 4.1% compared to a flat topology operation. In conclusion, PACO-CLS demonstrates strong scalability properties up to N = 100 nodes through optimized complexity management and hierarchical extensions, confirming its suitability for large-scale, multi-patient monitoring systems.

Real-time hardware implementation

To rigorously evaluate the practical feasibility of PACO-CLS, we deployed and profiled the protocol on actual low-power WBAN hardware, specifically the MySignals HW V3 platform (featuring an 8-bit ATMega328 microcontroller clocked at 16 MHz, with 32 KB Flash and 2 KB SRAM) for the sensor nodes, and a Raspberry Pi 4 for the Fog layer. This section details the computational overhead, memory footprint, and energy consumption on the resource-constrained sensor nodes, alongside a rigorous statistical validation of the comparative results.

Computational overhead and time complexity

The execution time per routing cycle (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{exec}}$$\end{document}) on the ATMega328 is dominated by three sequential processes: posture classification via Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM), link stability prediction via the Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model, and the Ant Colony Optimization (ACO) path search. The total execution time is formulated as: 279\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{exec}} = T_{\mathrm{GMM}} + T_{\mathrm{VAR}} + T_{\mathrm{ACO}}.$$\end{document}

Empirical profiling using AVR-GCC instrumentation yields \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{GMM}} \approx 0.08$$\end{document} ms (evaluating 5 classes with 16-dimensional features), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{VAR}} \approx 0.32$$\end{document} ms (sparse matrix-vector multiplication for L=4 link metrics), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{ACO}} \approx 5.3$$\end{document} ms (forward exploration and backward pheromone update for A=100 ants). Thus, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_{\mathrm{exec}} \approx 5.7$$\end{document} ms.

Given that PACO-CLS operates on a proactive routing decision interval of Δ t = 100 ms (aligned with 10 Hz physiological sampling), the CPU utilization is: 280\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_{\mathrm{CPU}} = \frac{T_{\mathrm{exec}}}{\Delta t} \times 100\% = \frac{5.7}{100} \times 100\% = 5.7\%.$$\end{document}

This leaves over 94% of the CPU capacity available for critical sensing and BLE communication tasks, proving that the predictive overhead is well within real-time constraints.

Memory footprint and hybrid storage

The strict 2 KB SRAM limit of the ATMega328 necessitates careful memory management. The theoretical memory requirement for the pheromone matrix (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2N^2$$\end{document}), ant memory (2AN), and routing tables exceeds the SRAM capacity for N > 15. To resolve this, we implemented a hybrid memory architecture. The large, static pheromone matrix and historical VAR coefficients are stored in the 32 KB Flash memory. During a routing cycle, only the active ant’s traversal stack, the current node’s routing table, and the VAR state vector are paged into SRAM. The active SRAM usage is strictly bounded by: 281\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_{\mathrm{SRAM}} = M_{\mathrm{vars}} + M_{\mathrm{stack}} + M_{\mathrm{active\_ant}} = 32 + 64 + 96 = 192 \mathrm{bytes}.$$\end{document}

This optimization ensures that PACO-CLS operates comfortably within the 2048-byte SRAM limit, even for dense topologies (N=20).

Energy consumption

The energy consumed by the microcontroller during the routing cycle is calculated based on the active current draw (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\mathrm{active}} = 1.5$$\end{document} mA in optimized low-power mode) and the supply voltage (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V_{\mathrm{dd}} = 3.3$$\end{document} V): 282\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\mathrm{comp}} = T_{\mathrm{exec}} \cdot V_{\mathrm{dd}} \cdot I_{\mathrm{active}} = 5.7 \times 10^{-3} \cdot 3.3 \cdot 0.0015 \approx 28.2~\mu\mathrm{J}.$$\end{document}

For context, transmitting a single 512-bit physiological packet via BLE requires approximately \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{\mathrm{tx}} = 76.8~\mu\mathrm{J}$$\end{document}. Therefore, the computational energy overhead of PACO-CLS is merely \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$28.2 / 76.8 \approx 36.7\%$$\end{document} of a single transmission, and because it prevents multiple retransmissions, the net energy balance is highly positive.

Statistical validation and confidence intervals

To ensure the robustness of our performance claims, all simulation metrics reported in this study are derived from n = 1000 independent Monte Carlo trials. We report the 95% Confidence Intervals (CIs) calculated as: 283\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathrm{CI}_{95\%} = \bar{x} \pm z_{\alpha/2} \frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{n}},$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{x}$$\end{document} is the sample mean, σ is the standard deviation, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z_{0.025} = 1.96$$\end{document}. Furthermore, to verify that the performance improvements of PACO-CLS over baseline protocols are statistically significant and not due to random variance, we applied the non-parametric Kruskal-Wallis H-test. The test statistic is computed as: 284\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H = \frac{12}{N_{\mathrm{tot}}(N_{\mathrm{tot}}+1)} \sum_{i=1}^{k} \frac{R_i^2}{n_i} - 3(N_{\mathrm{tot}}+1),$$\end{document}

where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\mathrm{tot}} = 4000$$\end{document} is the total number of observations across k=4 protocols, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_i = 1000$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R_i$$\end{document} is the sum of ranks for the i-th protocol.

Hardware implementation feasibility and statistical significance of PACO-CLS

As detailed in Table 12, while LEACH-WBAN exhibits a lower per-cycle CPU time (2.1 ms), its reactive nature requires a much shorter decision interval (200 ms) and incurs massive control overhead for cluster head rotation, resulting in higher overall energy drain. Conversely, Q-Learning requires a very short decision interval (20 ms) to maintain policy convergence, leading to a prohibitive CPU utilization of 62.0% and high SRAM usage (1,024 bytes) to store the Q-table.

PACO-CLS, by contrast, achieves a balanced CPU utilization of 5.7% and a minimal SRAM footprint of 192 bytes. Most importantly, the Kruskal-Wallis test yields p < 0.001 across all key metrics (Network Lifetime, Energy Efficiency, PDR, Latency, and SAR), confirming with 99.9% confidence that the improvements delivered by PACO-CLS are statistically significant. These hardware and statistical validations conclusively demonstrate that PACO-CLS is not only theoretically superior but also practically deployable on ultra-low-power, resource-constrained wearable devices.

Conclusion and future works

This paper presents PACO-CLS, a novel routing protocol for Wireless Body Area Networks that synergizes bio-inspired Ant Colony Optimization, predictive analytics, and thermal-aware mechanisms to achieve ultra-reliable, energy-efficient, and bio-safe communication. By leveraging a Vector Autoregressive (VAR) model to forecast correlated link stability based on dynamic physiological and channel metrics, PACO-CLS proactively mitigates motion-induced fading and RF-induced tissue heating, yielding significant performance gains over state-of-the-art baselines (QC-TriL, LEACH-WBAN, and Q-Learning) in NS-3.40 simulations calibrated with MySignals HW V3 empirical data. Specifically, the protocol achieves a network lifetime of 32.4 ± 1.2 hours (a 16.5–40.3% improvement), an energy efficiency of 17.8 ± 0.2 Mb/J (a 38.0–56.1% increase), and a packet delivery ratio of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$98.5\% \pm 0.3\%$$\end{document}, while strictly maintaining an average Specific Absorption Rate (SAR) below 1.0 W/kg and end-to-end latency under 50 ms, thereby satisfying stringent clinical QoS requirements. Sensitivity analysis confirms that the predictive stability metric is the dominant driver of this performance, accounting for up to 56% of the variance reduction, while hardware profiling validates its real-time deployability on resource-constrained microcontrollers with negligible computational overhead. Future work will focus on enhancing the resilience and scalability of PACO-CLS in extreme environments through advanced AI paradigms. First, we plan to replace the linear VAR predictor with deep learning architectures, such as Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, deployed at the Fog layer to capture non-linear, long-term dependencies in human mobility and channel dynamics. Second, to support distributed, multi-patient healthcare systems (e.g., smart hospitals), we will integrate a Federated Learning (FL) framework. This will allow multiple WBANs to collaboratively train the posture classification and link prediction models without sharing raw, sensitive physiological data, thereby ensuring strict patient privacy while improving global model generalization. Additionally, we will explore adaptive modulation (BPSK/QPSK switching) and IEEE 802.15.6-compliant frequency hopping to further tighten latency bounds and ensure sustained bio-safety, ultimately positioning PACO-CLS as a foundational, AI-driven framework for next-generation smart healthcare applications.
